<a id='question-135'></a>

### Question 135: Gross Anatomy: Upper Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 135
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the axilla, specifically the brachial plexus and axillary artery, and their potential injury patterns related to shoulder dislocations, particularly anterior dislocations. (理解腋窝内肱丛和腋动脉的解剖关系，以及与肩关节脱位相关的潜在损伤模式，特别是前脱位。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male rugby player presents to the emergency department after a forceful tackle during a match. He reports immediate, severe pain in his left shoulder and inability to move his arm. On examination, his left shoulder appears visibly deformed, with the arm held in slight abduction and external rotation. Palpation reveals a prominent bulge anteriorly in the left axilla. Neurological examination reveals complete loss of sensation over the lateral aspect of his left shoulder (the "regimental badge" area) and weakness in shoulder abduction and external rotation. Motor examination shows intact elbow flexion and wrist extension. Axillary artery pulses are diminished but palpable. Anteroposterior (AP) and scapular Y radiographic views confirm an anterior dislocation of the left glenohumeral joint. Which structure is most likely compressed or injured due to this anterior shoulder dislocation?

(A) Median nerve
(B) Ulnar nerve
(C) Axillary artery
(D) Radial nerve
(E) Posterior humeral circumflex artery

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism:** Forceful tackle leading to shoulder injury.
- **Presentation:** Severe left shoulder pain, inability to move the arm, visible deformity (arm abducted and externally rotated). This clinical picture strongly suggests an anterior shoulder dislocation.
- **Physical Exam:** Prominent bulge anteriorly in the axilla. This suggests displacement of structures normally located posterior to the humeral head, now positioned anteriorly due to the dislocation.
- **Neurological Exam:** Loss of sensation over the lateral shoulder ("regimental badge" area) points to injury of the superior lateral cutaneous nerve of the arm, a branch of the posterior cord of the brachial plexus. Weakness in shoulder abduction (deltoid muscle) and external rotation (teres minor, infraspinatus muscles) further implicates the axillary nerve (also from the posterior cord).
- **Motor Exam:** Intact elbow flexion (musculocutaneous nerve, anterior cord) and wrist extension (radial nerve, posterior cord) help localize the lesion.
- **Vascular Exam:** Diminished axillary artery pulses suggest compression or injury to the artery.
- **Radiology:** Confirms anterior glenohumeral dislocation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is most likely compressed or injured.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Anterior Shoulder Dislocation:** The most common type (95%), where the humeral head moves anteriorly and inferiorly relative to the glenoid fossa.
- **Axillary Anatomy:** The axillary artery and brachial plexus cords pass through the axilla. The axillary artery runs deep to the pectoralis minor muscle, transitioning into the brachial artery at the inferior border of the teres major muscle. The posterior cord of the brachial plexus (giving rise to the axillary and radial nerves) runs posterior to the axillary artery.
- **Pathophysiology:** In an anterior dislocation, the humeral head is displaced anteriorly. Structures normally posterior to the humeral head (like the posterior cord of the brachial plexus and the axillary artery) are stretched or compressed as they are forced anteriorly over the displaced humeral head.
- **Axillary Nerve Injury:** The axillary nerve wraps around the surgical neck of the humerus and innervates the deltoid and teres minor muscles. It also provides sensory innervation to the skin over the lateral shoulder (regimental badge area). Injury is common in anterior dislocations.
- **Axillary Artery Injury:** The axillary artery is vulnerable to compression or even rupture as it is stretched over the displaced humeral head. The diminished pulses noted in the vignette support this possibility.
- **Other Nerves:** The median and ulnar nerves (anterior cord) are less commonly injured in anterior dislocations as they are more medial and anterior. The radial nerve (posterior cord) can be injured, but the axillary nerve is more frequently affected.
- **Posterior Humeral Circumflex Artery:** This artery branches from the axillary artery and accompanies the axillary nerve, supplying the deltoid and shoulder joint. While potentially affected, direct compression of the main axillary artery is a more significant and common vascular complication.

##### 3. Embryological Considerations:**
- The brachial plexus develops from the ventral rami of spinal nerves C5-T1. The cords (anterior, middle, posterior) form within the axilla. The axillary artery develops from the 7th intersegmental artery. Their close proximity within the axilla makes them vulnerable to injury together.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Median nerve: Originates from the anterior cord (C8, T1). It runs more medially in the arm and is less likely to be directly compressed by the humeral head in an anterior dislocation. Injury typically occurs more distally (e.g., carpal tunnel syndrome) or proximally with penetrating trauma.
- (B) Ulnar nerve: Originates from the anterior cord (C8, T1). It runs medially in the arm and is even less likely to be injured in an anterior shoulder dislocation compared to the median nerve. Injury typically occurs at the elbow (cubital tunnel) or wrist (Guyon's canal).
- (D) Radial nerve: Originates from the posterior cord (C5-T1). While also from the posterior cord like the axillary nerve, it runs more posteriorly and laterally in the arm (in the radial groove of the humerus). It is less commonly injured in anterior shoulder dislocations than the axillary nerve, although it can be injured with humeral shaft fractures or posterior dislocations. The vignette describes sensory loss in the regimental badge area and weakness in abduction/external rotation, classic signs of axillary nerve injury, not radial nerve injury (which would cause wrist drop and sensory loss over the dorsum of the hand).
- (E) Posterior humeral circumflex artery: This artery accompanies the axillary nerve and supplies the deltoid. While it might be compromised due to its association with the axillary nerve, the main axillary artery is the larger vessel at risk of significant compression or injury leading to diminished pulses, as described in the vignette. Injury to the main axillary artery is a more critical vascular complication than injury to this smaller branch.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location in Axilla                                  | Relationship to Humeral Head in Anterior Dislocation | Clinical Findings                                                                 | Nerve Origin |
| :------------------------ | :-------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------- |
| **Axillary Artery (Correct)** | Deep to pectoralis minor, posterior to brachial plexus cords | Stretched/Compressed anteriorly over displaced head    | Diminished pulses, potential thrombosis/rupture                                   | N/A          |
| Axillary Nerve            | Posterior cord, wraps around surgical neck           | Stretched/Compressed anteriorly over displaced head    | Weakness in abduction/external rotation, sensory loss over lateral shoulder        | Posterior Cord |
| Median Nerve              | Anterior cord, medial aspect                          | Less likely affected                                 | Weakness in forearm pronation, wrist/finger flexion, sensory loss in hand          | Anterior Cord |
| Ulnar Nerve               | Anterior cord, medial aspect                          | Less likely affected                                 | Weakness in finger abduction/adduction, sensory loss in medial hand/little finger | Anterior Cord |
| Radial Nerve              | Posterior cord, runs laterally/posteriorly in arm    | Less likely affected (more common in posterior disloc) | Weakness in wrist/finger extension, sensory loss over dorsum of hand             | Posterior Cord |
| Post. Hum. Circumflex Artery | Accompanies Axillary Nerve                          | Potentially affected with Axillary Nerve              | Potential compromise to deltoid blood supply, less critical than main artery injury | Axillary Artery |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Anterior shoulder dislocation commonly injures the axillary nerve (due to its course around the surgical neck) and can compress the axillary artery as it passes over the displaced humeral head. Remember the clinical signs: arm abducted/externally rotated, regimental badge sensory loss, deltoid/teres minor weakness, and potential vascular compromise. Differentiate this from posterior dislocations (arm adducted/internally rotated, often associated with radial nerve injury) and inferior dislocations (luxatio erecta).

<a id='question-136'></a>

### Question 136: Gross Anatomy: Upper Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 136
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the axilla, specifically the brachial plexus and axillary artery, and their potential injury during shoulder dislocation, leading to specific neurological deficits. (理解腋窝内肱丛和腋动脉的解剖关系，以及肩脱位时它们可能受损，导致特定的神经功能缺损。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male presents to the emergency department after falling onto his outstretched right arm while skiing. He complains of severe right shoulder pain and inability to abduct his arm. Physical examination reveals a visibly deformed right shoulder, consistent with an anterior dislocation. Neurovascular assessment shows intact radial and ulnar pulses, but sensory examination reveals numbness over the lateral aspect of his right shoulder (the "regimental badge" area). Motor examination demonstrates significant weakness in shoulder abduction and external rotation, but elbow flexion and wrist extension are intact. An axillary nerve block is administered for pain management. Which nerve is most likely injured in this patient?

(A) Median nerve
(B) Ulnar nerve
(C) Musculocutaneous nerve
(D) Axillary nerve
(E) Radial nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism of Injury:** Fall onto outstretched arm, leading to anterior shoulder dislocation. This mechanism is a classic cause of axillary nerve injury.
- **Symptoms:** Severe shoulder pain, inability to abduct the arm. Abduction is primarily initiated by the supraspinatus muscle (innervated by the suprascapular nerve) and continued by the deltoid muscle (innervated by the axillary nerve). Weakness in abduction strongly suggests deltoid involvement.
- **Physical Exam Findings:**
    - Anterior shoulder dislocation: Confirms the traumatic event.
    - Intact radial and ulnar pulses: Rules out major vascular injury to the axillary artery or its branches.
    - Numbness over the lateral shoulder ("regimental badge" area): This sensory distribution is specific to the axillary nerve, which innervates the skin over the deltoid muscle.
    - Weakness in shoulder abduction and external rotation: Deltoid (abduction) and teres minor (external rotation) are innervated by the axillary nerve.
    - Intact elbow flexion and wrist extension: These functions are primarily mediated by the musculocutaneous nerve (biceps brachii, brachialis) and radial nerve (triceps brachii, wrist extensors), respectively, indicating these nerves are likely spared.
- **Conclusion:** The combination of anterior shoulder dislocation, weakness in abduction and external rotation, and sensory loss over the lateral shoulder points directly to an axillary nerve injury.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Axillary Nerve Anatomy:** The axillary nerve (C5, C6) arises from the posterior cord of the brachial plexus in the axilla. It passes posteriorly through the quadrangular space (bounded by teres minor, teres major, long head of triceps, and humerus) along with the posterior circumflex humeral artery. It then winds around the surgical neck of the humerus, innervating the deltoid muscle and teres minor muscle. It provides sensory innervation to the skin over the lateral shoulder (regimental badge area).
- **Relationship to Shoulder Dislocation:** Anterior shoulder dislocation often stretches or compresses the axillary nerve as it wraps around the surgical neck of the humerus. The nerve is particularly vulnerable in this location.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the axillary nerve results in paralysis or weakness of the deltoid (impaired abduction) and teres minor (impaired external rotation). Sensory loss occurs over the lateral shoulder. This clinical picture perfectly matches the patient's presentation.
- **Differential Diagnosis:**
    - **Musculocutaneous Nerve:** Innervates anterior compartment muscles of the arm (biceps, brachialis, coracobrachialis) and provides sensory innervation to the lateral forearm. Injury would cause weakness in elbow flexion and sensory loss in the lateral forearm.
    - **Radial Nerve:** Innervates posterior compartment muscles of the arm and forearm (triceps, wrist/finger extensors) and provides sensory innervation to the posterior arm, forearm, and dorsum of the hand. Injury would cause wrist drop and sensory loss in its distribution.
    - **Median Nerve:** Innervates most forearm flexors and thenar muscles, and provides sensory innervation to the palmar aspect of the thumb, index, middle, and radial half of the ring finger. Injury would cause weakness in thumb opposition/flexion and sensory loss in its distribution.
    - **Ulnar Nerve:** Innervates some forearm flexors (FCU, FDP medial half) and intrinsic hand muscles, and provides sensory innervation to the medial hand (little finger, medial half of ring finger). Injury would cause claw hand deformity and sensory loss in its distribution.
- **Embryology:** The brachial plexus develops from the ventral rami of spinal nerves C5-T1. The posterior cord, from which the axillary nerve arises, gives rise to nerves innervating the posterior compartment of the upper limb.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Median nerve: Incorrect. Injury would cause weakness in forearm pronation, wrist/finger flexion, and thumb opposition, along with sensory loss in the lateral 3.5 fingers palmar side. The patient has intact elbow flexion and wrist extension, and sensory loss is on the lateral shoulder, not the hand.
- (B) Ulnar nerve: Incorrect. Injury would cause weakness in wrist flexion/adduction, finger abduction/adduction, and intrinsic hand muscles (claw hand), along with sensory loss in the medial 1.5 fingers. The patient's deficits are in shoulder abduction/external rotation and lateral shoulder sensation.
- (C) Musculocutaneous nerve: Incorrect. Injury would cause weakness in elbow flexion (biceps, brachialis) and sensory loss over the lateral forearm. The patient has intact elbow flexion and sensory loss over the lateral shoulder.
- (E) Radial nerve: Incorrect. Injury would cause weakness in elbow extension (triceps) and wrist/finger extension (wrist drop), along with sensory loss over the posterior arm, forearm, and dorsum of the hand. The patient has intact elbow extension and wrist extension, and sensory loss is on the lateral shoulder.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Axillary Nerve (Correct) | Musculocutaneous Nerve | Radial Nerve        | Median Nerve          | Ulnar Nerve           |
|----------------------|--------------------------|------------------------|---------------------|-----------------------|-----------------------|
| **Origin**           | Posterior Cord (C5, C6)  | Lateral Cord (C5, C6, C7)| Posterior Cord (C5-T1)| Lateral/Medial Cords | Medial Cord (C8, T1)  |
| **Motor Innervation**| Deltoid, Teres Minor     | Biceps, Brachialis, Coracobrachialis | Triceps, Wrist/Finger Extensors | Forearm Flexors, Thenar | FCU, FDP (medial), Intrinsic Hand |
| **Sensory Innervation**| Lateral Shoulder         | Lateral Forearm        | Posterior Arm/Forearm, Dorsum Hand | Palmar Lateral 3.5 Fingers | Medial 1.5 Fingers, Medial Hand |
| **Key Deficit**      | Weak Abduction, External Rotation, Lateral Shoulder Numbness | Weak Elbow Flexion, Lateral Forearm Numbness | Wrist Drop, Posterior Hand Numbness | Weak Thumb Opposition, Palmar Numbness | Claw Hand, Medial Hand Numbness |
| **Vulnerability**    | Surgical Neck of Humerus | None specific          | Spiral Groove, Radial Tunnel | Carpal Tunnel, Pronator Teres | Cubital Tunnel       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Anterior shoulder dislocation is a common cause of axillary nerve injury due to its course around the surgical neck of the humerus. Clinically, this presents as weakness in shoulder abduction (deltoid) and external rotation (teres minor), along with sensory loss over the lateral shoulder ("regimental badge"). Differentiating axillary nerve injury from other brachial plexus injuries requires careful assessment of motor and sensory deficits in specific muscle groups and dermatomes.

<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: Gross Anatomy: Upper Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 137
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the cubital fossa, specifically the course of the median nerve relative to the brachial artery and the potential for injury during procedures or trauma, and differentiating it from other structures. (理解肘窝内解剖关系，特别是正中神经相对于肱动脉的走行，以及在操作或外伤中受伤的潜力，并将其与其他结构区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a direct blow to his left elbow during a fall. He complains of immediate, severe pain and numbness in his forearm and hand. Physical examination reveals a visible deformity over the medial aspect of the elbow, consistent with a fractured medial epicondyle of the humerus. Neurovascular assessment shows intact radial pulse but diminished sensation in the palmar aspect of the thumb, index finger, middle finger, and the radial half of the ring finger. Motor examination demonstrates weakness in thumb opposition and abduction, as well as difficulty flexing the index and middle fingers at the distal interphalangeal joints. Anteroposterior and lateral radiographs confirm the medial epicondyle fracture, with displacement of fragments. The patient is scheduled for open reduction and internal fixation (ORIF). During the surgical exposure, the surgeon notes significant bleeding and identifies a laceration of a major nerve structure deep to the brachial artery, proximal to its bifurcation.

Which nerve is MOST likely lacerated in this patient?

(A) Ulnar nerve
(B) Radial nerve
(C) Axillary nerve
(D) Median nerve
(E) Musculocutaneous nerve

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism:** Direct blow to the elbow, medial epicondyle fracture with displacement. This points to trauma in the cubital fossa region.
- **Symptoms:** Numbness in the palmar aspect of the thumb, index, middle, and radial half of the ring finger. This sensory distribution corresponds to the median nerve.
- **Motor Deficits:** Weakness in thumb opposition and abduction (thenar muscles), difficulty flexing the index and middle fingers at the DIP joints (lumbricals 1 & 2, interossei). These motor functions are primarily innervated by the median nerve.
- **Surgical Findings:** Laceration of a major nerve structure deep to the brachial artery, proximal to its bifurcation, in the context of a medial epicondyle fracture. The median nerve runs deep to the brachial artery in the cubital fossa, just proximal to the artery's bifurcation into the radial and ulnar arteries. A medial epicondyle fracture can displace fragments medially, potentially injuring structures in the cubital fossa.
- **Key Clue Synthesis:** The specific sensory and motor deficits strongly implicate the median nerve. The location of the injury (deep to the brachial artery, proximal to its bifurcation, associated with medial epicondyle fracture) is consistent with the anatomical course of the median nerve in the cubital fossa.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Cubital Fossa Anatomy:** The cubital fossa is a triangular depression on the anterior aspect of the elbow. Its boundaries are the brachialis and pronator teres muscles (inferiorly), the brachioradialis muscle (laterally), and the pronator teres and flexor carpi ulnaris muscles (medially).
- **Median Nerve Course:** The median nerve enters the cubital fossa between the heads of the pronator teres muscle. It runs deep to the brachial artery, medial to the biceps tendon, and superficial to the pronator teres muscle. Proximal to the bifurcation of the brachial artery, the median nerve is typically located deep to the artery. Distal to the bifurcation, it passes between the two heads of the pronator teres. It gives off the superior subscapular nerve (motor to subscapularis) and the lateral cutaneous nerve of the forearm before entering the cubital fossa. In the forearm, it is accompanied by the brachial artery (initially) and then the ulnar artery. It passes through the carpal tunnel at the wrist.
- **Brachial Artery Course:** The brachial artery enters the cubital fossa from above, passing deep to the biceps tendon and brachialis muscle. It lies superficial to the median nerve proximally, then deep to it. It bifurcates into the radial and ulnar arteries just distal to the elbow joint, typically at the level of the neck of the radius.
- **Ulnar Nerve Course:** The ulnar nerve enters the cubital fossa posterior to the medial epicondyle of the humerus. It runs superficial to the flexor carpi ulnaris muscle and deep to the subcutaneous fascia in the cubital fossa, medial to the brachial artery. It is vulnerable to injury in the cubital fossa (e.g., hitting the "funny bone").
- **Radial Nerve Course:** The radial nerve typically does not pass through the cubital fossa. It runs in the radial groove of the humerus, then spirals around the humerus laterally, passing posterior to the lateral epicondyle before dividing into superficial and deep branches.
- **Axillary Nerve Course:** The axillary nerve runs through the quadrangular space in the axilla and innervates the deltoid and teres minor muscles. It is not located near the cubital fossa.
- **Musculocutaneous Nerve Course:** The musculocutaneous nerve pierces the coracobrachialis muscle and runs between the biceps brachii and brachialis muscles, supplying them. It terminates as the lateral cutaneous nerve of the forearm. It is not located near the cubital fossa.
- **Medial Epicondyle Fracture:** Fractures of the medial epicondyle can damage the ulnar nerve (which passes posterior to it) or, with significant displacement, structures within the cubital fossa, including the median nerve and brachial artery. The median nerve's position deep to the brachial artery makes it vulnerable to injury during procedures or trauma involving the artery or the medial aspect of the fossa.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's sensory loss (thumb, index, middle, radial half of ring finger) and motor weakness (thenar muscles, lumbricals 1&2) are classic signs of median nerve injury. The location of the injury described during surgery (deep to the brachial artery, proximal to bifurcation) precisely matches the anatomical course of the median nerve in the cubital fossa.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Ulnar nerve: The ulnar nerve runs posterior to the medial epicondyle and medial to the brachial artery in the cubital fossa. Injury typically causes sensory loss in the little finger and ulnar half of the ring finger, and weakness in intrinsic hand muscles (except thenar muscles) and wrist/finger flexors (FCU, FDP to digits 4&5). While medial epicondyle fractures can injure the ulnar nerve, the sensory and motor deficits described in the vignette are characteristic of the median nerve, not the ulnar nerve.
- (B) Radial nerve: The radial nerve does not pass through the cubital fossa. It runs laterally around the humerus. Injury typically causes wrist drop (weakness of wrist and finger extensors) and sensory loss over the posterior forearm and dorsum of the hand (excluding the fingertips). This pattern does not match the patient's presentation.
- (C) Axillary nerve: The axillary nerve is located in the shoulder region (axilla, quadrangular space). Injury typically causes weakness of shoulder abduction (deltoid) and external rotation (teres minor), along with sensory loss over the lateral shoulder (regimental badge area). This is anatomically distant from the elbow injury.
- (E) Musculocutaneous nerve: The musculocutaneous nerve runs in the anterior compartment of the arm, supplying the biceps brachii, brachialis, and coracobrachialis. Injury causes weakness in elbow flexion and supination, and sensory loss over the lateral forearm. This does not match the patient's hand sensory and motor deficits.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Median Nerve (Correct)                                  | Ulnar Nerve (Distractor A)                               | Radial Nerve (Distractor B)                               | Axillary Nerve (Distractor C)                            | Musculocutaneous Nerve (Distractor E)                     |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|
| **Course in Cubital Fossa** | Deep to brachial artery (proximal), between heads of pronator teres | Posterior to medial epicondyle, medial to brachial artery | Does not pass through cubital fossa                       | Located in axilla/shoulder region                         | Runs in anterior arm compartment, not cubital fossa        |
| **Sensory Distribution** | Palmar thumb, index, middle, radial half of ring finger | Little finger, ulnar half of ring finger, medial hand     | Dorsum of hand (radial 3.5 digits), posterior forearm     | Lateral shoulder                                           | Lateral forearm                                           |
| **Motor Innervation** | Thenar muscles, lumbricals 1&2, interossei, FDP (digits 2&3) | Intrinsic hand muscles (except thenar), FCU, FDP (digits 4&5) | Wrist/finger extensors, supinator                         | Deltoid, Teres Minor                                       | Biceps Brachii, Brachialis, Coracobrachialis              |
| **Vulnerability**     | Cubital fossa trauma, carpal tunnel syndrome           | Cubital fossa trauma (posterior to epicondyle), Guyon's canal | Spiral groove fracture, Saturday night palsy               | Shoulder dislocation, surgical neck fracture              | Anterior arm trauma                                       |
| **Relation to Brachial Artery** | Deep to artery (proximal to bifurcation)            | Medial to artery                                           | N/A                                                       | N/A                                                       | N/A                                                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The median nerve runs deep to the brachial artery in the proximal cubital fossa, just before the artery bifurcates. Trauma or surgical procedures in this region, especially involving the medial epicondyle, can injure the median nerve, leading to characteristic sensory loss in the lateral 3.5 fingers and motor weakness in the thenar muscles and lateral lumbricals. Differentiating median nerve injury from ulnar nerve injury is crucial based on the specific sensory and motor deficits. Remember the mnemonic "FOOSH" (Fall On OutStretched Hand) can cause median nerve injury at the wrist (carpal tunnel) or elbow (cubital fossa).

<a id='question-138'></a>

### Question 138: Gross Anatomy: Upper Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 138
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the cubital fossa, specifically the course of the median nerve relative to the brachial artery and the potential for injury during procedures or trauma, and differentiating it from other structures in the region. (理解肘窝内解剖关系，特别是正中神经相对于肱动脉的走行，以及在操作或外伤中受伤的潜力，并将其与其他区域结构区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a direct blow to his left elbow during a fall while skiing. He complains of immediate, severe pain in the forearm and hand, followed by numbness and tingling in the thumb, index finger, middle finger, and the radial half of the ring finger. On physical examination, his left forearm is diffusely swollen and tender to palpation, particularly over the anterior aspect of the elbow. Motor examination reveals weakness in wrist flexion, thumb opposition, and abduction of the thumb. Sensory examination confirms decreased sensation in the distribution of the median nerve distal to the wrist. An X-ray of the elbow reveals a non-displaced fracture of the distal humerus. The physician is concerned about potential nerve injury. Which of the following structures is most likely injured in this patient?

(A) Ulnar nerve as it passes posterior to the medial epicondyle
(B) Radial nerve as it passes between the brachialis and brachioradialis muscles
(C) Median nerve as it passes between the heads of the pronator teres muscle
(D) Brachial artery as it passes superficial to the biceps brachii tendon
(E) Axillary nerve as it passes through the quadrangular space

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism of Injury:** Direct blow to the elbow (fall while skiing) suggests trauma to the structures within or around the cubital fossa.
- **Symptoms:** Immediate severe pain, numbness/tingling in the thumb, index, middle, and radial half of the ring finger (median nerve distribution).
- **Physical Exam Findings:** Swelling and tenderness over the anterior elbow, weakness in wrist flexion (median nerve - flexor digitorum superficialis, flexor pollicis longus), thumb opposition (median nerve - opponens pollicis), and thumb abduction (median nerve - abductor pollicis brevis). Sensory loss in the median nerve distribution.
- **Imaging:** Non-displaced distal humerus fracture. While the fracture itself could cause nerve injury, the specific sensory and motor deficits point strongly to the median nerve.
- **Localization:** The combination of symptoms and signs strongly implicates injury to the median nerve in the region of the elbow. The question asks for the *most likely* site of injury based on the clinical presentation.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Median Nerve Course:** The median nerve is formed by the union of the lateral and medial cords of the brachial plexus in the axilla. It descends through the arm, initially lateral to the brachial artery, then crossing anteriorly to lie medial to it in the distal arm. In the cubital fossa, the median nerve passes *deep* to the bicipital aponeurosis and *between the two heads* of the pronator teres muscle. It then continues into the forearm.
- **Cubital Fossa Anatomy:** The cubital fossa is a triangular depression on the anterior aspect of the elbow. Its boundaries are the brachialis and biceps brachii muscles superiorly, the pronator teres muscle inferiorly, and the brachioradialis muscle laterally. The floor is formed by the brachialis and supinator muscles. The median nerve, brachial artery, and superficial veins of the forearm pass through the cubital fossa. The median nerve is the most medial of these structures as they pass distally through the fossa.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Trauma to the elbow, especially a direct blow or fracture, can injure the median nerve. The specific location of injury often dictates the pattern of sensory and motor deficits. Injury *at the elbow* (e.g., between the heads of the pronator teres) typically affects the forearm muscles innervated by the median nerve (flexor digitorum superficialis, flexor carpi radialis, pronator quadratus, pronator teres) and the hand muscles (thenar muscles, lumbricals 1&2, interossei). The patient's symptoms (weakness in wrist flexion, thumb opposition/abduction, sensory loss in the median distribution) are consistent with a median nerve injury at or distal to the elbow. Option (C) describes the precise anatomical location where the median nerve is vulnerable in the cubital fossa.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Ulnar nerve as it passes posterior to the medial epicondyle:** The ulnar nerve passes *posterior* to the medial epicondyle in the cubital tunnel. Injury here (e.g., compression, fracture) typically causes numbness/tingling in the little finger and the ulnar half of the ring finger, and weakness in intrinsic hand muscles (interossei, hypothenar muscles, adductor pollicis, lumbricals 3&4), and wrist/finger flexion (flexor carpi ulnaris, flexor digitorum profundus to digits 4&5). This does not match the patient's presentation.
- (B) **Radial nerve as it passes between the brachialis and brachioradialis muscles:** The radial nerve typically branches into the superficial and deep branches in the spiral groove of the humerus or just distal to it. It does *not* pass between the brachialis and brachioradialis in the cubital fossa. The superficial branch provides sensation to the dorsum of the hand, and the deep branch (posterior interosseous nerve) innervates the forearm extensors. Injury here would cause wrist drop and sensory loss on the dorsum of the hand, not the patient's symptoms.
- (D) **Brachial artery as it passes superficial to the biceps brachii tendon:** The brachial artery passes *deep* to the biceps brachii tendon in the upper arm and then becomes more superficial in the cubital fossa, lying lateral to the median nerve. Injury to the brachial artery would cause signs of vascular compromise (pallor, pulselessness, pain, paresthesia, paralysis, poikilothermia) in the forearm and hand, not the specific neurological deficits described.
- (E) **Axillary nerve as it passes through the quadrangular space:** The axillary nerve arises from the posterior cord of the brachial plexus and passes through the quadrangular space (bordered by teres minor, teres major, long head of triceps, and humerus) to innervate the deltoid and teres minor muscles and provide sensation to the skin over the deltoid region (regimental badge area). Injury here typically occurs with shoulder dislocations or proximal humerus fractures and causes deltoid weakness and sensory loss over the lateral shoulder, not the patient's forearm/hand symptoms.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location in Cubital Fossa/Elbow Region                                  | Innervation/Function Affected by Injury                                                                                                | Sensory Distribution Affected by Injury                                                                                                | Clinical Presentation Match? |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| Median Nerve          | Between heads of pronator teres; deep to bicipital aponeurosis          | Wrist flexion, thumb opposition/abduction, finger flexion (digits 2-3), pronation                                                     | Thumb, index, middle, radial half of ring finger (palmar aspect)                                                                   | **YES**                     |
| Ulnar Nerve           | Posterior to medial epicondyle (cubital tunnel)                           | Wrist/finger flexion (digits 4-5), intrinsic hand muscles (interossei, hypothenar, adductor pollicis), finger flexion (digits 4-5) | Little finger, ulnar half of ring finger (palmar and dorsal aspects)                                                                | NO                          |
| Radial Nerve          | Branches in spiral groove; superficial branch in forearm (dorsal hand)   | Wrist/finger extension, supination (deep branch/PIN)                                                                                   | Dorsum of hand (thumb, index, middle, radial half of ring finger)                                                                 | NO                          |
| Brachial Artery       | Lateral to median nerve in cubital fossa                                 | Vascular supply to forearm and hand                                                                                                | N/A (Vascular)                                                                                                                   | NO                          |
| Axillary Nerve        | Passes through quadrangular space (shoulder region)                       | Deltoid muscle, teres minor muscle                                                                                                 | Skin over lateral shoulder (regimental badge area)                                                                                 | NO                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The median nerve is vulnerable to injury in the cubital fossa, particularly as it passes between the heads of the pronator teres muscle. Injury here results in sensory loss in the palmar aspect of the thumb, index, middle, and radial half of the ring finger, and motor weakness affecting wrist flexion, thumb opposition/abduction, and flexion of the index and middle fingers. Differentiating median nerve injury from ulnar and radial nerve injuries relies on recognizing the specific sensory and motor deficits. Remember the mnemonic "FOOSH" (Flexion, Opposition, Opposition, Supination, Hand) for median nerve function in the hand and forearm.

<a id='question-139'></a>

### Question 139: Gross Anatomy: Upper Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 139
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the cubital fossa, specifically the course of the median nerve and its vulnerability to compression or injury, and differentiating it from other structures in the region. (理解肘窝内解剖关系，特别是正中神经的走行及其易受压迫或损伤的部位，并将其与其他区域结构区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male construction worker presents to the emergency department complaining of numbness and tingling in his thumb, index finger, middle finger, and the radial half of his ring finger. The symptoms began acutely after he struck his elbow forcefully against a concrete wall while working. On physical examination, his elbow is slightly swollen and tender to palpation over the anterior aspect, specifically just distal to the medial epicondyle. He exhibits weakness in thumb opposition and abduction, and difficulty with the "OK" sign (pinch grip between thumb and index finger). Sensory testing reveals decreased sensation over the palmar aspect of the thumb, index, middle, and radial half of the ring finger. Motor testing shows weakness in the thenar muscles (abductor pollicis brevis, opponens pollicis, flexor pollicis brevis superficial head). Deep tendon reflexes in the upper limb are normal. An X-ray of the elbow reveals no fracture.

Which structure is most likely compressed or injured in this patient?

(A) Ulnar nerve in the cubital tunnel
(B) Radial nerve in the spiral groove
(C) Median nerve as it passes between the heads of the pronator teres muscle
(D) Brachial artery distal to the elbow bifurcation
(E) Axillary nerve as it passes through the quadrangular space

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male construction worker - suggests potential for trauma.
- **Mechanism of Injury:** Forceful blow to the elbow against a concrete wall - points to injury in the elbow region.
- **Symptoms:** Numbness/tingling in thumb, index, middle, radial half of ring finger - classic sensory distribution of the median nerve in the hand.
- **Physical Exam Findings:**
    - Swelling/tenderness over anterior elbow, distal to medial epicondyle - localizes the injury to the anterior elbow region.
    - Weakness in thumb opposition/abduction, difficulty with "OK" sign - indicates weakness of thenar muscles innervated by the median nerve.
    - Decreased sensation in median nerve distribution - confirms sensory involvement of the median nerve.
    - Normal reflexes - suggests the injury is distal to the nerve roots and brachial plexus.
- **Imaging:** No fracture - rules out bony injury as the primary cause of nerve compression.
- **Conclusion:** The clinical presentation strongly points to an injury affecting the median nerve in the elbow region, causing sensory and motor deficits consistent with its distribution.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Median Nerve Course in the Elbow:** The median nerve enters the forearm by passing between the two heads (humeral and ulnar) of the pronator teres muscle in the cubital fossa. This location makes it vulnerable to compression or injury from direct trauma to the anterior elbow.
- **Median Nerve Innervation:**
    - **Sensory:** Palmar aspect of the thumb, index, middle, and radial half of the ring finger; dorsal aspect of the distal phalanges of these fingers.
    - **Motor:** Most forearm flexors (except pronator teres, which is innervated by the median nerve *before* it passes between the heads of pronator teres), thenar muscles (opponens pollicis, abductor pollicis brevis, superficial head of flexor pollicis brevis), and the first two lumbricals.
- **Patient's Deficits:** The patient's sensory loss (thumb, index, middle, radial ring finger) and motor weakness (thenar muscles, "OK" sign) precisely match the distribution of the median nerve distal to its branching in the forearm.
- **Location of Injury:** The tenderness distal to the medial epicondyle, anteriorly, corresponds to the location where the median nerve passes between the heads of the pronator teres. Compression here is a common cause of median nerve symptoms.
- **Differential Diagnosis:**
    - **Carpal Tunnel Syndrome:** Affects the median nerve at the wrist, causing similar sensory symptoms but typically sparing the forearm flexors and often involving nocturnal symptoms. The injury mechanism (elbow trauma) makes this less likely.
    - **Pronator Syndrome:** Specifically refers to median nerve compression *between the heads of the pronator teres*. This fits the clinical picture perfectly.
    - **Ulnar Nerve Injury:** Affects the little finger and ulnar half of the ring finger, and intrinsic hand muscles (except thenar muscles and first two lumbricals).
    - **Radial Nerve Injury:** Affects wrist and finger extension, and sensation over the dorsum of the hand (radial side).
    - **Brachial Artery Injury:** Would cause signs of vascular compromise (pallor, pulselessness, pain, paresthesia, paralysis, poikilothermia) and potentially hematoma formation, not the specific sensory/motor pattern described.
    - **Axillary Nerve Injury:** Affects the deltoid and teres minor muscles, causing shoulder abduction weakness and sensory loss over the lateral shoulder (regimental badge area).

##### 3. Embryological Considerations (胚胎发生学考点)
- The median nerve, like other peripheral nerves, develops from the ventral rami of spinal nerves and migrates distally. Its specific course through the limb is determined by its relationship to developing muscles and fascia. Understanding its path helps predict where it might be vulnerable.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Ulnar nerve in the cubital tunnel:** The ulnar nerve passes posterior to the medial epicondyle in the cubital tunnel. Injury here causes numbness/tingling in the little finger and ulnar half of the ring finger, and weakness of intrinsic hand muscles (except thenar muscles) and wrist/finger flexion (FCU, FDP to digits 4/5). This does not match the patient's sensory or motor deficits.
- (B) **Radial nerve in the spiral groove:** The radial nerve is most commonly injured in the spiral groove of the humerus (e.g., humeral shaft fracture). This causes wrist drop (inability to extend wrist and fingers) and sensory loss over the dorsum of the hand (radial side). This does not match the patient's presentation.
- (D) **Brachial artery distal to the elbow bifurcation:** Injury to the brachial artery would cause signs of vascular compromise (e.g., diminished pulse, pallor, coolness) and potentially ischemia. While trauma can injure the artery, the specific sensory and motor deficits described are characteristic of nerve injury, not vascular injury. The artery bifurcates into radial and ulnar arteries distal to the elbow, but injury *there* wouldn't cause the median nerve distribution symptoms.
- (E) **Axillary nerve as it passes through the quadrangular space:** The axillary nerve innervates the deltoid and teres minor muscles and provides sensation to the lateral shoulder. Injury typically occurs with shoulder dislocation or proximal humerus fracture. Symptoms include shoulder abduction weakness and sensory loss over the "regimental badge" area. This is anatomically distant from the elbow injury and does not match the patient's symptoms.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location of Injury                               | Sensory Deficit                                                                 | Motor Deficit                                                                                                | Associated Symptoms/Signs                                                                 |
| :--------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Median Nerve (Pronator Teres)** | Between heads of pronator teres (elbow)           | Thumb, index, middle, radial 1/2 ring finger (palmar)                            | Thenar muscles (opposition, abduction), forearm flexors (most), FPL, FDP (index/middle), lumbricals 1 & 2 | Weak "OK" sign, weak thumb opposition/abduction, sensory loss in median distribution      |
| Ulnar Nerve (Cubital Tunnel)   | Posterior to medial epicondyle (elbow)            | Little finger, ulnar 1/2 ring finger (palmar & dorsal)                          | Intrinsic hand muscles (except thenar, lumbricals 1&2), FCU, FDP (ring/little)                               | Claw hand (hyperextension MCP, flexion IP), weak grip, sensory loss in ulnar distribution |
| Radial Nerve (Spiral Groove)   | Spiral groove of humerus (proximal arm)           | Dorsum of hand (radial side), dorsal aspect of thumb, index, middle fingers | Wrist drop, finger drop, weak finger/thumb extension                                                           | Wrist drop, sensory loss on dorsum of hand                                               |
| Axillary Nerve (Quadrangular Space) | Axilla (shoulder region)                         | Lateral shoulder ("regimental badge")                                          | Deltoid (shoulder abduction), Teres Minor (external rotation)                                                 | Shoulder abduction weakness, sensory loss over lateral shoulder                            |
| Brachial Artery (Distal Elbow) | Distal to elbow bifurcation                       | None (vascular)                                                                 | None (vascular)                                                                                              | Pallor, pulselessness, pain, paresthesia, paralysis, poikilothermia (6 P's)                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The median nerve is vulnerable to compression as it passes between the heads of the pronator teres muscle in the cubital fossa. Trauma to the anterior elbow can cause "Pronator Syndrome," resulting in sensory loss in the median nerve distribution of the hand and weakness of the thenar muscles and forearm flexors. Differentiating median nerve injury at the elbow from carpal tunnel syndrome (wrist injury) and ulnar nerve injury (different sensory/motor distribution) is crucial.

<a id='question-140'></a>

### Question 140: Gross Anatomy: Upper Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 140
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the axilla, specifically the brachial plexus and axillary artery, and their potential injury patterns related to humeral neck fractures. (理解腋窝内肱丛和腋动脉的解剖关系，以及颈骨折相关的潜在损伤模式。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after a fall from standing height, complaining of severe right shoulder pain and inability to move his right arm. He has a history of osteoporosis. Physical examination reveals significant deformity of the right proximal humerus and marked tenderness over the surgical neck. Neurological examination shows complete paralysis of shoulder abduction and external rotation, with sensory loss over the lateral aspect of the upper arm (the "regimental badge" area). Radial pulse is absent on the right side. Anteroposterior and lateral radiographs confirm a displaced fracture of the surgical neck of the right humerus. Which structure is most likely injured concurrently with this fracture?

(A) Ulnar nerve
(B) Median nerve
(C) Axillary artery branches distal to the profunda brachii artery
(D) Axillary vein
(E) Long thoracic nerve

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with osteoporosis (increased fracture risk).
- **Mechanism:** Fall from standing height leading to right shoulder pain and immobility.
- **Physical Exam:** Deformity and tenderness over the proximal humerus (surgical neck).
- **Neurological Deficit:** Paralysis of shoulder abduction and external rotation (deltoid and teres minor muscles). Sensory loss over the lateral upper arm (regimental badge area, supplied by the superior lateral cutaneous nerve of the arm). This pattern points to an injury of the axillary nerve (C5, C6 roots).
- **Vascular Deficit:** Absent radial pulse indicates occlusion or severe compromise of the axillary artery or its major branches.
- **Radiographic Finding:** Displaced fracture of the surgical neck of the humerus.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is *most likely* injured concurrently.

The combination of a surgical neck fracture, axillary nerve palsy (deltoid/teres minor paralysis, sensory loss in regimental badge area), and absent radial pulse strongly suggests injury to structures closely associated with this fracture site. The axillary nerve and the axillary artery (and its branches) wrap around the surgical neck of the humerus.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Surgical Neck of Humerus:** The region just distal to the tubercles (greater and lesser) and proximal to the deltoid tuberosity. It is a common site for fractures, especially in older individuals with osteoporosis.
- **Axillary Nerve:** Arises from the posterior cord of the brachial plexus (C5, C6 roots). It passes posteriorly through the quadrangular space (along with the posterior circumflex humeral artery) and winds around the surgical neck of the humerus. It innervates the deltoid and teres minor muscles and provides sensory innervation to the skin over the lateral shoulder (via the superior lateral cutaneous nerve of the arm). Its close proximity to the surgical neck makes it vulnerable to injury in fractures at this location.
- **Axillary Artery:** The axillary artery is the direct continuation of the subclavian artery, beginning at the lateral border of the first rib and ending at the inferior border of the teres major muscle, where it becomes the brachial artery. It passes through the axilla, deep to the clavicle, pectoralis minor, and teres major muscles. It gives off several branches.
- **Branches of the Axillary Artery:**
    - **Superior Thoracic Artery:** Arises from the first part of the axillary artery, supplying the upper thoracic wall.
    - **Thoracoacromial Artery:** Arises from the second part, supplying the pectoral muscles, acromion, and deltoid.
    - **Lateral Thoracic Artery:** Arises from the second part, supplying the serratus anterior and pectoral muscles.
    - **Subscapular Artery:** Arises from the third part, supplying the scapular region.
    - **Anterior Circumflex Humeral Artery:** Arises from the third part, accompanies the posterior circumflex humeral artery around the surgical neck, supplying the shoulder joint.
    - **Posterior Circumflex Humeral Artery:** Arises from the third part, accompanies the anterior circumflex humeral artery around the surgical neck, supplying the shoulder joint and deltoid muscle.
    - **Profunda Brachii Artery (Deep Brachial Artery):** The largest branch, arising from the third part. It runs posteriorly with the radial nerve through the triangular space, winding around the humerus in the radial groove. It supplies the triceps and posterior compartment muscles of the arm.
- **Relationship to Fracture:** A displaced surgical neck fracture can directly injure the axillary nerve as it wraps around the bone. It can also injure the axillary artery or its branches, particularly the anterior and posterior circumflex humeral arteries, which encircle the neck of the humerus. The profunda brachii artery is located more distally in the radial groove, making it less likely to be injured by a surgical neck fracture itself, although it can be injured by associated distal humeral fractures or dislocations.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's presentation (surgical neck fracture, deltoid/teres minor paralysis, sensory loss in regimental badge area, absent radial pulse) indicates injury to both the axillary nerve and the axillary artery. The question asks which *structure* is most likely injured concurrently. The axillary artery gives rise to several branches, including the anterior and posterior circumflex humeral arteries which wrap around the surgical neck. Injury to the main axillary artery or its proximal branches (like the circumflex humeral arteries) is common with surgical neck fractures and would explain the absent radial pulse. The profunda brachii artery is a major branch, but it is located more distally. Therefore, injury to the axillary artery *branches* proximal to the profunda brachii artery is the most likely concurrent vascular injury explaining the absent radial pulse in the context of a surgical neck fracture.

##### 3. Embryological Considerations (胚胎发生学考点)
- The brachial plexus develops from the ventral rami of spinal nerves C5-T1. The axillary nerve originates from the posterior cord (C5, C6). The axillary artery develops from the 7th intersegmental artery. The close anatomical relationship in the adult reflects their shared developmental pathway and migration patterns within the limb bud.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Ulnar nerve: The ulnar nerve originates from the medial cord (C8, T1) of the brachial plexus. It travels down the medial aspect of the arm, passes posterior to the medial epicondyle of the humerus, and enters the forearm. It is not in close proximity to the surgical neck of the humerus and is therefore unlikely to be injured by this type of fracture. Injury typically occurs at the elbow (cubital tunnel syndrome) or wrist.
- (B) Median nerve: The median nerve originates from the medial and lateral cords (C5-T1) of the brachial plexus. It travels down the medial aspect of the arm, passes anterior to the medial epicondyle, and enters the forearm. Like the ulnar nerve, it is not closely associated with the surgical neck of the humerus. Injury can occur in the carpal tunnel at the wrist or sometimes in the arm, but not typically with a surgical neck fracture.
- (D) Axillary vein: The axillary vein is formed by the union of the basilic and cephalic veins in the axilla. It runs deep to the axillary artery and passes inferiorly through the axilla. While it is in the axilla, it is generally medial to the surgical neck of the humerus and less likely to be directly injured by the fracture itself compared to the artery or nerve wrapping around the bone.
- (E) Long thoracic nerve: The long thoracic nerve arises from the roots of the brachial plexus (C5, C6, C7). It runs along the lateral chest wall, deep to the serratus anterior muscle, which it innervates. Injury to this nerve causes "winging" of the scapula. It is located far from the surgical neck of the humerus and is not associated with this type of fracture.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location relative to Surgical Neck | Innervated Muscle(s) / Sensory Area | Arterial Supply | Brachial Plexus Origin | Likelihood of Injury with Surgical Neck Fracture |
| :------------------------ | :-------------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :-------------- | :--------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ |
| **Axillary Nerve**        | Wraps around posterior aspect      | Deltoid, Teres Minor; Regimental Badge | None           | Posterior Cord (C5, C6) | **High**                                          |
| **Axillary Artery**       | Wraps around (circumflex arteries) | N/A                                 | Supplies arm   | N/A                   | **High** (especially branches)                      |
| Ulnar Nerve               | Medial arm, elbow                 | Intrinsic hand muscles, medial 1.5 fingers | None           | Medial Cord (C8, T1)    | Low                                               |
| Median Nerve              | Medial arm, forearm               | Anterior forearm muscles, lateral hand | None           | Medial/Lateral Cords (C5-T1) | Low                                               |
| Axillary Vein             | Axilla, medial to artery          | N/A                                 | Drains arm     | N/A                   | Low                                               |
| Long Thoracic Nerve       | Lateral chest wall                | Serratus Anterior                  | None           | Roots (C5-C7)          | Low                                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Fractures of the surgical neck of the humerus commonly injure the axillary nerve (due to its close proximity wrapping around the bone) and the axillary artery or its branches (particularly the circumflex humeral arteries). Remember the clinical presentation: deltoid/teres minor paralysis, sensory loss over the lateral shoulder, and potential vascular compromise (absent radial pulse). Differentiate this from injuries at other levels of the humerus (e.g., mid-shaft fractures injuring the radial nerve in the spiral groove, supracondylar fractures injuring the median nerve).

<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Gross Anatomy: Upper Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 141
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the cubital fossa, specifically the course of the median nerve and its vulnerability to compression or injury, and differentiating it from other structures in the region. (理解肘窝内解剖关系，特别是正中神经的走行及其易受压迫或损伤的部位，并将其与其他区域结构区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male construction worker presents to the emergency department complaining of numbness and tingling in his thumb, index finger, middle finger, and the radial half of his ring finger. The symptoms began acutely after he struck his elbow forcefully against a concrete block while working. On physical examination, his elbow is slightly swollen and tender to palpation over the medial aspect of the antecubital fossa. Motor examination reveals weakness in thumb opposition and abduction, as well as difficulty with the "OK" sign (pinch grip between thumb and index finger). Sensory examination confirms decreased sensation in the distribution described above. Tinel's sign is positive over the medial aspect of the elbow. An X-ray of the elbow shows no fracture. Which of the following structures is most likely injured?

(A) Ulnar nerve
(B) Radial nerve
(C) Brachial artery
(D) Median nerve
(E) Axillary nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male construction worker (suggests potential for trauma).
- **Mechanism of Injury:** Acute onset after forceful impact to the elbow (trauma).
- **Symptoms:** Numbness and tingling in thumb, index, middle, and radial half of ring finger (specific sensory distribution).
- **Physical Exam:** Swelling and tenderness over the medial antecubital fossa (localization of injury).
- **Motor Deficits:** Weakness in thumb opposition/abduction, difficulty with "OK" sign (specific motor deficits).
- **Sensory Deficits:** Decreased sensation in the specific distribution mentioned above (correlates with motor deficits).
- **Provocative Test:** Positive Tinel's sign over the medial elbow (suggests nerve irritation/injury).
- **Imaging:** X-ray negative for fracture (rules out bony injury as the primary cause of neurological symptoms).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely injured structure.

The combination of sensory loss in the median nerve distribution (lateral 3.5 fingers), motor weakness involving thenar muscles (thumb opposition/abduction) and the FPL muscle (tested by the "OK" sign), tenderness over the medial elbow, and a positive Tinel's sign strongly points towards an injury to the median nerve as it passes through the cubital fossa.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Median Nerve Course:** The median nerve is formed by the union of the lateral and medial cords of the brachial plexus in the axilla. It descends through the arm, passing anterior to the brachial artery and biceps brachii tendon, entering the cubital fossa. In the cubital fossa, it lies medial to the brachial artery and lateral to the ulnar nerve. It passes between the two heads of the pronator teres muscle to enter the forearm.
- **Cubital Fossa Anatomy:** The cubital fossa is a triangular depression on the anterior aspect of the elbow. Its boundaries are the brachioradialis muscle laterally, the pronator teres muscle medially, and an imaginary line connecting the medial and lateral epicondyles superiorly. The floor is formed by the brachialis and supinator muscles. The contents include the brachial artery, median nerve, radial nerve (deep branch), and the biceps brachii tendon.
- **Median Nerve Function:** The median nerve provides sensory innervation to the palmar aspect of the thumb, index, middle, and radial half of the ring finger, as well as the dorsal aspect of the distal phalanges of these fingers. It provides motor innervation to the forearm muscles (pronator teres, pronator quadratus, flexor carpi radialis, palmaris longus, flexor digitorum superficialis) and the thenar muscles (abductor pollicis brevis, flexor pollicis brevis, opponens pollicis) via the recurrent motor branch.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the median nerve in the cubital fossa, often due to compression (e.g., pronator syndrome) or direct trauma, results in sensory loss in its distribution and weakness of the muscles it innervates, matching the patient's presentation. The "OK" sign tests the flexor pollicis longus (FPL), which is innervated by the anterior interosseous nerve (a branch of the median nerve). Thumb opposition and abduction are primarily mediated by the thenar muscles, also innervated by the median nerve. The location of tenderness and positive Tinel's sign over the medial elbow further support median nerve involvement.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Ulnar nerve:** The ulnar nerve passes posterior to the medial epicondyle in the cubital fossa (often in the groove), medial to the median nerve and brachial artery. Injury typically causes sensory loss in the little finger and medial half of the ring finger, and weakness of intrinsic hand muscles (interossei, hypothenar muscles), leading to clawing of the 4th and 5th digits and inability to adduct the fingers. The patient's sensory and motor deficits do not match the ulnar nerve distribution.
- (B) **Radial nerve:** The radial nerve bifurcates in the cubital fossa into superficial (sensory) and deep (motor) branches. The superficial branch provides sensation to the posterior arm, forearm, and dorsal hand (radial side). The deep branch (posterior interosseous nerve) innervates the forearm extensors. Injury typically causes wrist drop (weakness of wrist and finger extensors) and sensory loss over the dorsum of the hand. The patient's symptoms are primarily sensory and motor deficits in the anterior compartment of the forearm and the hand, not the posterior compartment.
- (C) **Brachial artery:** The brachial artery is the main arterial supply to the upper limb, passing through the cubital fossa medial to the biceps tendon and lateral to the median nerve. Injury would cause significant hemorrhage, distal ischemia, and potentially limb-threatening compromise, presenting with pulselessness, pallor, paresthesia, paralysis, and pain (the 5 Ps). While trauma could injure the artery, the primary presentation described (specific sensory/motor deficits without signs of severe ischemia) points away from the artery being the *most likely* injured structure causing these specific neurological symptoms.
- (E) **Axillary nerve:** The axillary nerve arises from the posterior cord of the brachial plexus and innervates the deltoid and teres minor muscles, and provides sensation over the lateral shoulder (regimental badge area). Injury typically occurs with shoulder dislocation or proximal humerus fracture, causing shoulder abduction weakness and sensory loss over the lateral shoulder. This nerve is located much more proximally and its function is unrelated to the patient's elbow injury and hand symptoms.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure        | Location in Cubital Fossa                                  | Sensory Distribution (Hand)                                  | Motor Function (Hand/Forearm)                                     | Clinical Presentation (Injury)                                                                 |
|-----------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Median Nerve     | Medial to brachial artery, lateral to ulnar nerve          | Palmar thumb, index, middle, radial half of ring finger; dorsal distal phalanges | Thenar muscles (opposition, abduction), FPL (flexion), FDS, FCR, pronators | Sensory loss (lateral 3.5 fingers), thenar atrophy, weak "OK" sign, pronator syndrome          |
| Ulnar Nerve      | Medial to median nerve, posterior to medial epicondyle     | Medial half of ring finger, little finger, medial dorsal hand | Intrinsic hand muscles (interossei, hypothenar), FCU, FDP (medial 2 digits) | Sensory loss (medial 1.5 fingers), claw hand, weak finger adduction, cubital tunnel syndrome |
| Radial Nerve     | Divides into superficial/deep branches in cubital fossa    | Dorsal hand (radial side), posterior arm/forearm              | Forearm extensors (wrist drop), supinator, brachioradialis         | Wrist drop, sensory loss on dorsum of hand, Saturday night palsy                               |
| Brachial Artery  | Medial to biceps tendon, lateral to median nerve          | N/A (Artery)                                                 | N/A (Artery)                                                      | Hemorrhage, ischemia, pulselessness, pallor, paralysis, pain (5 Ps)                           |
| Axillary Nerve   | Proximal (axilla/shoulder)                                | Lateral shoulder                                              | Deltoid (abduction), Teres Minor (external rotation)             | Shoulder abduction weakness, sensory loss over lateral shoulder                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of sensory loss in the lateral 3.5 fingers and weakness of thenar muscles/FPL following elbow trauma strongly suggests median nerve injury in the cubital fossa. Differentiating median nerve injury from ulnar and radial nerve injuries relies on understanding their specific sensory and motor distributions in the hand and forearm, as well as their anatomical relationships within the cubital fossa. Remember the mnemonic "FOOSH" (Flexor pollicis longus, Opponens pollicis, OK sign, Sensory lateral 3.5 fingers, Hand intrinsics) for median nerve function.
